2026 midterms: Overview · Senate · House · Governors · Scorecard

2026 House Forecast

All 435 seats, state by state, seat by seat. A probabilistic model: every district starts from its 2024 presidential margin on the 2026 lines plus the national swing and incumbency, then blends in 257 individual district polls where they exist. 172 competitive or polled districts were fact-checked. No district-specific judgment calls. Data as of 2026-11-03.

Certain = decided by the ballot. Democrats' Certain + Safe + Likely + Lean seats total 216.

Big picture. The generic ballot is about D+8.7, against R+2.6 in the 2024 House vote. Mid-decade redistricting shifted roughly 9 notional seats to Republicans, but a swing that size outweighs it. Republicans become favorites only if the polls and generic ballot miss by about 3.6+ points in their direction, beyond the baseline shading. Polling misses of that size have happened (2014, 2020), which is why this is about 87%, not a certainty.

Try your own scenario

Move the levers to see how control changes. The page re-runs 10,000 simulations in your browser on every change, using the same model and the same random draws each time, so any change you see comes from the lever.

Call too-close-to-call races yourself, state by state

39 districts in 21 states are too close to call: the model gives either side between 20% and 80%. Use All D / All R to call a state's close districts at once, or open a state to call them one by one. Everything you don't call stays simulated. Each state line shows its expected Democratic seats across all its districts.

Method, parameters and sources
  • Fundamentals. 2024 presidential margin on the 2026 lines + national shift +9.2 (generic ballot D+8.7 minus the 2024 national presidential margin R+1.5 = +10.2, shaded -1 for the generic ballot's history of overstating Democrats) × elasticity (1 − 0.25×|margin|/40, capped at 40) + incumbency 3 pts for a member running (×0.5 on mostly new territory). Where the member's 2024 race was on comparable lines, 50% of their over/under-performance vs. the presidential margin (net of generic incumbency) carries forward. District error SD: 5.5 (incumbent), 6 (open), 6.5 (incumbent on new lines).
  • District polls. Same weighting as the Senate model (recency half-life 21 days, sample size, LV/RV, ×0.5 and a 4.5-pt shift for campaign/party internals). A district poll average carries residual error SD 4.5 plus sampling noise, and is blended with the fundamentals by precision.
  • Why the generic ballot is shaded when Senate and governor polls are not. The two rest on different evidence. Senate and governor polls have missed in both directions, and one cycle's miss says almost nothing about the next (correlation 0.17 between cycles, 1998–2022), so those models assume no bias and test misses as scenarios. The generic ballot is a single national question asked the same way every cycle, and its final average overstated Democrats in 7 of 10 cycles from 2006 to 2024, by 1.85 points on average (standard error about 0.7). That is a measured property of the instrument, not a guess about this year, so half of it (-1) is applied to every district. The full and zero versions are in the scenario table.
  • Correlated error. National SD 3, regional SD 2, district SD as above; Student-t, 8 df.
  • Ballot-decided seats (one major party absent, or a California top-two race within one party) are certain.
  • Tiers: Safe ≥ 99%, Likely ≥ 90%, Lean ≥ 75%, Tilt ≥ 60%, else Toss-up. The 6-point rule only flags deep dives (the original sheet used 10).
  • Not modeled: Louisiana's Dec 12 runoffs (the Nov 3 leader is shown); candidate scandals or money beyond what polls capture.
  • national_sd: Final-2-week generic-ballot average vs national House vote, 2006-2024: RMSE 2.84, SD 2.27 (538 generic_topline_historical.csv)
  • generic_ballot_bias_adj: That average overstated Democrats by 1.85 pts, in 7 of 10 cycles (standard error ~0.7). Half is applied; the full and zero versions are scenarios
  • regional_sd: Census-region share of district error ~1.0-1.3; state-level clustering ~3.1 (538 election-results recompute); 2.0 used
  • fundamentals_sd: Residual SD of partisan lean + national swing + incumbency in competitive seats 6.2, ~5.5 with past overperformance (2016-2024 panel recompute)
  • fundamentals_sd_open: Measured 5.5 on only 86 open seats; set to 6.0 since open seats are less predictable (538)
  • fundamentals_sd_new: 2022 redistricting year had the highest incumbent residual SD, 6.5
  • poll_state_sd: Nonpartisan district-poll error net of the national miss, 4.2-5.8 (538 raw_polls 2006-2022, final 21 days)
  • single_poll_sd: Within-race spread of independent district polls 4.9-5.8
  • partisan_shift: Released internals lean toward the sponsor by 4.4 (D) to 5.6 (R); 4.6-4.8 vs independent polls of the same race; 538 assumes 4
  • incumbency_bonus: Regression estimates 4.0-6.1 are inflated by selection; Split Ticket ~3 (2024), Annotations 2.6; 3.0 used
  • overperformance_shrink: 0.38-0.71 of a member's overperformance persisted on the same lines; not applied on substantially new lines
  • elasticity_k: Swing near-uniform to a lean of ~30, 0.5-0.9x in the most partisan seats; fitted k 0.17-0.77; 0.25 used
  • t_df: Fundamentals residuals have excess kurtosis 1.23 (implies ~9 df); 8 used
  • national_shift: Generic ballot D+8.7 (Oct 2026 average) minus 2024 national presidential margin R+1.5
  • overperf_cap: Judgment: 2024 results of 60+ points (usually uncontested) are ignored, and carried-forward overperformance is capped at ±15 so one odd race can't dominate

Seats changing hands, by the party of the sitting member (new seats with no incumbent are not tagged).

Running; district redrawn to Republican lean (Black population ~50% -> ~40%)

Context — not used in the forecast

D primary: Figures unopposed in Aug 11 special primary

R primary: Marques 50.0% vs Harris 21.7% (+28.3), Aug 11 special primary, no runoff

  • Mid-cycle redraw turned a Harris +7 seat into Trump +15; Figures is a first-term incumbent on mostly new turf
  • Money: Figures raised $2.71M with $1.20M cash on hand vs Marques $1.72M and $178K (FEC, Oct 14)
  • Trump and the NRCC endorsed Marques, a former state rep; Marques turned down debates while Figures accepted three
  • Democratic internals show a tie; Sabato calls Figures an underdog anyway

Earlier call (v1, judgment-based)

Rhett Marques (R, Tilt) — A Trump +15 baseline and unanimous Lean R ratings favor Marques. Figures has incumbency, a large cash edge and a favorable 2026 environment. But the only polls are Democratic internals showing a tie, and those usually overstate their side. The seat probably flips, narrowly.

Alaska 1 seat · leaders D 0 – R 1 · expected D seats 0

At-large seat with ranked-choice voting. Democrats withdrew and back independent Bill Hill.

AK-AL Nick Begich III Bill Hill Nick Begich III 2024 pres R +13.1 · 11 polls R +1.7 R +1.7± 6.7
R 61%		 Deep dive Tilt RNick Begich III · 61%
Evidence: AK-AL

How the number is built

11 district polls average R +1.7 after removing sponsor lean (unadjusted R +1.3); the polls get 100% of the weight. Ranked-choice. Independent Bill Hill is the main challenger; nominal Democrat Hafner polls 5-9% and his voters mostly rank Hill next. Hill has not said which party he would caucus with, so a Hill win is counted as an independent, not a Democratic seat. Forecast R +1.7, total error SD 6.7 → Republican wins 61% of simulations.

Incumbent running

Context — not used in the forecast

D primary: Top-four (Aug 18): Hafner 3.8%; Matt Schultz (D) 8.1% withdrew and endorsed Hill

R primary: Top-four: Begich 44.6% (1st), +12.1 over Bill Hill (I, 32.5%)

  • Independent Bill Hill (Bristol Bay fisherman, ex-superintendent) is the de facto Dem-backed challenger: DCCC Red to Blue, AFL-CIO, Schultz endorsement; Dem candidates withdrew to consolidate
  • RCV: Hafner (D) and McDermott (L) transfers matter; DFP shows 50-50 final round
  • Money edge Begich: ~$5.5M raised vs Hill ~$1.3M (through July); Cook moved to Lean R Oct 23
  • Peltola running for Senate on same ballot may boost Dem-leaning turnout

Earlier call (v1, judgment-based)

Nick Begich III (R, Lean) — Begich leads every first-choice poll (by 1-12) and most simulated RCV final rounds (53-47 in ASR and Siena), with a large money edge in a Trump+13 state. Hill's consolidation of the anti-Begich vote and the D environment make it closer than the baseline, and Cook just moved it to Lean R, but the incumbent remains favored.

  • Coover, a veterinarian and ex-Army pilot, campaigned with Jon Tester; Schmidt rallied with Trump on Oct 19

Earlier call (v1, judgment-based)

Derek Schmidt (R, Likely) — The only poll is a DCCC internal showing Schmidt +3, which qualifies the race as competitive, but internal polls usually overstate the sponsor's side. At Trump+20, with an incumbent holding a money edge, Schmidt remains a clear favorite even in a D-leaning environment.

Matthew Dunlap (D, Tilt) — The research agent leaned LePage, but the evidence points the other way. This year the generic ballot is about D+9, an 11-point swing. The seat is R+9 on the 2024 presidential vote and all three raters say Lean R, so it's close, but LePage is a known quantity with a ceiling, and the polling plus the environment tip it to Dunlap.
